Rook looked appraisingly at Neil, nodded, and said gently, “Don’t want to chat this morning—no problem, I get it; we’ve all been there. Remember this: you need friends outside just like you did on the inside. If you ever want to chat, my name’s Rook.” 
With that, he put his head back and went to sleep.
#
Neil stared out the window, his eyes unfocused as his mind wandered, not noticing as the bus left the Outer’s grey suburbia, leaving behind block after block of squat apartment buildings, strip malls, and entertainment centres. 
The bus passed over the long viaduct that separated the Rust Belt factories and warehouses from the city’s edge. The viaduct was three lanes in each direction and arrow-straight for at least two miles as it stretched over a vast flood plain, the result of a past environmental catastrophe from before the Reclamation. 
Neil had never been here—it was always out of sight and out of mind for a Level 7 in his former life. Of course, he knew about the Rust Belt; it was storied history that the factories here had helped rebuild the Bi-Continental Government of the Americas after international trade collapsed. 
They had passed over the viaduct now into Rust Belt proper—miles upon miles of mega factories and warehouses, with business parks holding hundreds of smaller factories and warehouses. Unlike the grey and grime of the Outer’s suburbia, there was colour here; the buildings were bright and clean with corporate logos, company colours, and murals splashed across the huge walls. The lawns and gardens lining the driveways into each building were green and well-maintained. 
Corporations always want to look good, Neil thought. 
Rook was awake and had been watching Neil look out through the window, as if appraising him. He matched Neil’s gaze. “Yep, here it is—the beating heart of the country, the place that makes everything and saved us all. Look at all that money just oozing out of every building… and we here on this bus kept it all ticking along.” 
Neil didn’t respond; instead, he kept surveying the scenery. He recognised some of the names—they were suppliers he’d used. 
Rook didn’t let Neil’s lack of response stop him. “You know, without us 3’s and 4’s, nothing would’ve gotten made. The whole world needed us to be exactly where we were.” 
Neil wasn’t used to this kind of talk, and the inference annoyed him. He looked at Rook, who was smiling expansively. “That’s not true at all. I built things—lots of things—before…” 
Rook interrupted, ice in his eyes. “Sorry to burst your bubble, Neil, but you built things only because of the suppliers we created out here. Without us, you would’ve made exactly zero.” 
Neil froze. Rook’s use of his name was a shock. “I didn’t think I’d given you my name.” 
Rook’s steely eyes didn’t waver as he stared right through Neil. “Neil, I knew who you were before you even arrived at your apartment last night. In fact, the people I know watched your trial. Like I said, you need friends out here, and I think you and I can help each other.” 
Neil remembered how the bus had silenced when Rook mentioned his friends and was now genuinely worried. “Look, I’m just here to do my time in probation and get back out into the world without any trouble.” 
Rook laughed, the cold glint in his eyes never leaving. “Get back? What, to Level 7? You’re stuck here like the rest of us. You think you’ll be able to pay off your Housing Loan? Think they’ll let you get out of the grind? No, my new friend, they need us out here so they could sit in their shiny glass towers and sip champagne.” 
Neil wasn’t going to be stuck out here longer than necessary; he already had a plan in mind to get out. “Listen, Rook, is it? I don’t know about you, but I’m not some loser who’s stuck here. I’ll get back up and leave this place—and people like you—behind.” 
Rook shook his head in disappointment, like Neil had failed a test. “Five years in prison, and you’ve still got the Level 7 attitude. Piece of advice from someone who knows—keep that kind of talk under wraps.” 
The bus jerked to a stop, and the PA system barked mechanically, “Destination reached, all off the bus.” 
Neil started to get up, and Rook grabbed his arm—his grip was strong and practiced. Neil couldn’t break it in the confined space of the seat row. Rook leaned in and said tightly, “When you work out how fucked you are, I’ll be here.” 
Neil squeezed past, shook Rook’s hand off his arm, and marched off the bus with the rest of the passengers without looking back.
#
Neil got off the bus and looked around. They’d stopped at a small covered bus stop on one side of a wide cul-de-sac, big enough for double semi-trailers to turn around in. 
The group of passengers—Neil thought about thirty or so—milled about, chatting for a moment until it broke apart as groups of twos and threes split off and headed to various factories. He could see another bus coming up the road, ready to dislodge its group of workers. If Neil could have seen further, he’d have seen hundreds of similar buses ferrying workers in and out all day and night. 
Confused about what was next, Neil awkwardly stood at the bus stop. “Where the hell do I go?” he thought as he watched Rook walk away with a small group of workers to one of the factories. 
One of his fellow passengers, a robust shorter man who seemed to be dressed a bit better than the utilitarian work clothes everyone else, including Neil, wore, approached and shot out an enthusiastic hand to shake. “Neil? Neil Lucas?” 
Neil responded as the man shook his hand wildly. “Yes, that’s me.” 
“Excellent, I’m Knox. We didn’t get a chance to meet before you got on the bus… I’m your sponsor.” Knox, still grasping Neil’s hand, ushered him towards the only office building in the cul-de-sac. “This is us over here. You must have done something right—you’ve got an office job, just like me…” 
Neil, reclaiming his hand and walking with Knox towards the ten-storey glass office building, his mind trying to catch up, asked, “Sponsor? I’m sorry, I don’t know anything about that.” 
Knox sped up the pace and dragged Neil along. “They still told you absolutely nothing when you left prison—nothing ever changes. Don’t worry, we’ll have time to talk about all of it today whilst we get you settled in.” 
Knox and Neil were in the HR department waiting room on the second floor. It was stark and almost hospital-like; the walls were a uniform beige, dotted here and there with low-quality reprints of once-famous landscapes. Neil sat one seat away from Knox on a harsh and unforgiving plastic chair, one of about half a dozen against the wall. 
It was only Neil and Knox waiting. Neil figured by the wear on the carpet this was a busy department. 
Knox sat back, like he was preparing for a long wait, and started talking. “So, welcome to your new job. You hit the jackpot being qualified for an office job! I myself used to be an accountant before… all this.” He waved around the room. “I understand you were in construction before?” 
Neil answered, “Sort of. I used to own a construction business.” 
Knox said, “That’s close enough. From what I understand, you’ve been allocated a project management job here—not many of those going around.” 
“I’m not really a project manager,” said Neil. 
Knox said, “It doesn’t matter. Do your best.” 
Neil considered Knox’s statement. He wasn’t actually sure what he’d expected, but project management was something he probably could do—at least it wasn’t a factory job. 
Neil ventured, “You mentioned sponsor?” 
Knox smiled. “Yes, more tour guide and mentor—just someone who knows the ropes and can help get newbies like you settled into their new routine. Just think of me as your personal AI, since you can’t have one right now. Anything you need to know about life after prison, I’m here to help.” 
Knox’s tone changed. “Speaking of learning the ropes, what was that with Rook when you got off the bus?” 
Neil stiffened. He wasn’t sure anyone else had seen it. “Nothing, just a misunderstanding.” 
Knox pressed, “It didn’t look like nothing. Rook is not someone to have a problem with.” 
“You know him?” asked Neil. 
Knox looked around, assured they were alone, and spoke quietly. “Rook is part of the Greys. They are not people you want to be associated with.” 
“Who are the Greys? I’ve never heard of them,” Neil said, curious. 
Knox shifted over to the seat next to Neil and leaned in close, almost whispering. “One of those things you never heard about in the city. The Greys are a group who opposes the system we live under. They’re always looking for ways to break it. They are not nice, and quite a few people have been burnt by them—literally.” 
The thought of an under-the-radar group who wanted to break the system was a shock to Neil’s Level 7 view of the world. 
They were interrupted when the door to the office opened abruptly. A very well-dressed and presented lady barked, “Neil, which one of you is Neil Lucas?” 
Neil put his hand up. 
“Come with me,” she said as she retreated back into the office. 
Neil followed her into the office. It was large and housed half a dozen workstations, all occupied by office workers at their terminals or on the phone. The buzz of activity was a stark contrast to the quiet of the waiting room. 
She sat at her workstation and gestured to Neil to sit in the chair opposite her desk. 
“Sorry about that. Normally, the waiting room is full, and I have to make myself heard. I’m Eleanor. I’ll be getting you all set up with your new job.” 
Neil, sensing she was trying to be helpful, said, “Nice to meet you, Eleanor. I’m kind of in the dark about what’s going on here.” 
She laughed. “Well, I’m here to help you out.” Looking at her terminal, she clicked through a few screens. “It says here you used to own a construction company… perfect. We’ve got you a role as a Project Management Assistant here at Stratum Projects.” 
Eleanor saw Neil’s reaction. “Sorry, but that’s about as high as you can go being a Level 3. Actually, it’s better than I’ve seen most get—you must have some friends out there somewhere.” 
She continued, “So, I need to tell you this isn’t like a normal job. First, you’ve got to be here on time at 8am—don’t miss the bus—and you’ll finish at 6pm each night. You got Sundays off, and for the next 12 months, you’ll get no holidays.” 
Neil reacted angrily. “Ten hours a day, six days a week? I was better off in prison!” 
She moved forward over the top of Neil’s protest. “Now, if you were late, you’d get penalised points—same if your performance reviews were sub-standard—but this job will be easy enough for you, so you shouldn’t have an issue.” 
Neil interrupted, “…but I’ve done my time in prison. This seems completely unfair…” 
Eleanor shook her head at Neil. “I’m going to have to remind you—you’re a Probationary Citizen. Whilst you transition back to being an unrestricted citizen, you’re not completely free. You’d better understand that really quick.” 
She continued, any of her goodwill towards Neil now long gone. “So, as a Probationary Citizen, you were not entitled to any salary over and above your Baseline credit allowance. We don’t do overtime here, so there’ll be no extra work.” 
Eleanor checked a few boxes on the terminal and finally said, “That’s it, Mr Lucas. You can go now. Knox will get you situated in the office. If you have any questions about your employment here, be sure to address them with your direct manager. Goodbye and best of luck to you.” 
He was dismissed. 
Neil left the office without another word, fuming. The anger he’d pushed down last night flared back hotter than ever. “I’m just a fucking slave here,” he thought, as the realisation sank in that he might just be stuck here forever.
#
The first day went by in a flash, Neil wasn’t in the mood to be social but he forced himself to be. This was his life for the next twelve months one way or another so he’d just have to make the best of it.
Knox was a dutiful guide, he seemed to know everyone and wasn’t afraid to gossip about them behind their backs. Neil didn’t really listen, but he humoured Knox, Rook’s words “you need friends out here” still ringing in Neil’s ears.
The job was simple, Neil was going to be filing and doing general administrative jobs for the project management office. A glorified errand boy for people who knew far less about construction than Neil did.
Neil guessed they’d thought with his experience, he’d know his way around organising all the plans, manifests, quotes and surveys large construction projects would have. Thet weren’t wrong, he be able to the job with his eyes closed, and perhaps with a bit of spare time he could figure a way of getting out as quickly as possible.
He was one of five assistants to the project office, Knox had introduced him to all of them, but Neil had already forgotten thier names. Knox himself wasn’t one of them, he worked in different department completely.
Right on time the bus arrived at the stop, 6:30pm, the sun was setting in the late summer heat and everyone piled onto the bus. It was a different crowd, he only recognised a few faces. 
Neil sat and scanned the faces getting on he was relieved when he didn’t see Rook get on. 
Tully from the morning bus ride sat next to Neil, he hadn’t realised just how large Tully was in the morning, he was as tall as Neil, but he must have been at least 240 pounds of muscle.
Tully extended his hand “Hi, names Tully, didn’t catch yours this morning”
Neil accepted the handshake, it was a more gentle than he expected, the faint smell of oil and grease in grained in Tully’s hand hit Neil’s nose “Neil, good to meet you”
Tully asked “They’ve got you over in Stratum Projects, very cushy, everyone wants an office job” he spread his hands wide for Neil to see, the black line under his nails made it clear he got dirty for his job “How’d you swing that?”
Neil shook his head “I have absolutely no idea”
Tully laughed “I have a feeling you’ll find out soon enough… now if you don’t mind I’m going to get some shut eye, us factory workers don’t get to sit down all day in air-conditioned comfort”
Neil wanted to ask what Tully meant, but it was clear the conversation was over, he sat in silence for the rest of the ride, Tully softly snoring beside him.

















CHAPTER 6

DISRUPTIVE PERSON (JIM 3)

Citizenship Act (revised 2045)
Section 4, Subsection 4 – Temporary Status – (A) Disruptive Person
1.1 The designation of Disruptive Person can be applied to any citizenship level.
1.2 Such a designation will be applied to a Citizen who displays either: a single severe anti-social behaviour incident as reported by no fewer than 10 other citizens, or at least 3 incidents of anti-social behaviour as reported by no fewer than 5 other citizens.
1.3 A Citizen will be designated a Disruptive Person for a period of 12 months.
1.4 Designation as a Disruptive Person does not alter a Citizen’s status points or level.
1.5 A Disruptive Person will accrue penalty points at twice the normal rate for the period of the designation.
1.6 If a Disruptive Person has an anti-social incident as reported by no fewer than 5 other citizens during the 12-month period, the citizen will be automatically re-designated a Level 3 - Probationary Citizen (Temporary).
#
This was the day he’d been working towards almost his entire life, chasing down every points hack and social credit programme he could find. All the relentless networking and arse-kissing of higher levels had paid off.
Today was the day Jim left Level 7 behind and assumed his new life fully as a Level 8 Full Citizen. 
Today, Jim was leaving the Landis Corporation for good and starting as the new VP of Private Client Business at Nova Corp.
Sarah had been busy getting Jim’s wardrobe ready all week. Jim was impressed at how quickly she’d got the tailor to make his new suit; it had arrived just three days after the fitting. Helen had put her onto the tailor—the same one Shane used—and she was just as excited as Sarah was.
It was perfectly tailored to fit Jim’s lean but athletic six-foot frame, not a crease or seam out of place. It was real merino wool in a charcoal grey with a subtle pinstripe.
Sarah had matched it with a new real cotton white shirt and a gold silk tie.
“Gold for success and luck today,” she had said as he’d left for work.  
The news of his signing had hit Landis almost immediately, and they had arranged a big farewell that morning before his start at Nova Corp. Jim couldn’t help thinking that the farewell morning tea was as much for their benefit as his. He knew all too well how important making friends with higher levels was.  
Jim was relaxing with his EV in auto-drive, as was his habit, putting together in his head a few kind and humble words for his old team. His heart wasn’t in it—he really just wanted to move on and forget them. They’re just stepping stones, he thought, necessary evils on my path to the top. He admonished himself—he’d better make it good; there would be no more repeats of the arrogance he’d shown at Apex. That could have really blown up in his face. But I’m Level 8 now, I’m untouchable. Luckily, Shane’s points lawyer had made sure the incident never saw the light of day again.  
His phone rang. It was the car dealership.
“Hello,” Jim answered.
“Good morning, Mr Dalton. This is Jackson, the Sales Manager at Citadel Prestige.”
“Hi, Jackson, good to chat to you again. Have you got news?” Jim asked.
“I do, Mr Dalton. We’ve been able to find a current-year Lumora Model 9 in slate grey with a light tan interior; however, it will take about a week to get here from another dealer.”
“Excellent news. I’m glad you could find one—I was worried I’d have to buy a slot in the production line six months down the track!” Jim was beaming at this news.
“Well, we do aim to satisfy our clientele, Mr Dalton. If you’d like to drop into the dealership anytime in the next few days with your current vehicle, we’ll do an appraisal on it, and we can also sign the contract in person,” Jackson said smoothly.
“Sure, give me a day or two, and I’ll drop down,” said Jim.
“One last item, Mr Dalton. Could you please authorise the deposit so we can secure the vehicle? As you know, we will need a deposit of 50%, which is 100,000 credits.”
Jim sucked in a breath abruptly as the size of the deposit hit him. He’d only just received his first year’s 150,000 credits as a Level 8 a few days ago, and he hadn’t started at Nova Corp yet.
“Approved,” Jim said hesitantly. He’d never paid that much in one transaction—not even close.
“Thank you, Mr Dalton. The deposit has cleared. Look forward to seeing you in a few days.”
Ouch.
#
The offices of Landis Corp felt different that morning. It was odd how much his perception could change in only a week. What he used to think of as a reasonably well-appointed office space, where he had one of the better offices, just seemed worn and tacky now, like a veil had been lifted from his vision, showing him a reality he’d been avoiding.
Last week, Jim had been a valued member of the team, easily interacting with his peers, chatting with them with a relaxed ease, gossiping, joking, and talking business without any barrier.
Today, that ease had gone. He was not one of the team. His work colleagues seemed oddly subdued around him—not unfriendly, but the casual camaraderie was gone. Conversations he’d had a mere seven days ago were now forced, subtly calculated to curry favour with Jim in his new position.
Jim felt awkward, and he was glad this would be over soon. He just wanted to be done with this morning tea and move on to his new role at Nova Corp.  
He had been to his new office only a few days ago to pick out some of the decorations for it. Calling it an office almost didn’t do it justice. It was on the corner of the 45th floor, just three levels down from the Executive Clubhouse on the top floor of Nova’s new building. The space was at least three times larger than where he was sitting now, not counting the large marble-clad private bathroom complete with shower and walk-in wardrobe.
He had picked out some tasteful original seascapes for the walls under the watchful eye of his new Executive Assistant and was keen to see if they’d match the paint colour he’d chosen.
He had to close off his work here first, and daydreaming about an office he could be in right now wasn’t helping if he didn’t wrap up this waste of time.  
Jim had one final email prepped and ready to send before the gathering in the Carter Room in ten minutes to say his official goodbyes.
He sent it and logged off for the final time.
“Damn it,” he thought. He’d forgotten to attach his working files.
Jim tried to log back in to the terminal.
[User Not Found – Please Check Username or Password]
He hadn’t typed it wrong—after years, logging in was muscle memory now. He tried again.
[User Not Found – Please Check Username or Password]
Had they off boarded him already? Jim guessed it didn’t really matter; they’d find the data easily enough.  
His phone chimed with a new message.
It was from his Personal Citizenship Points Portal.
“Dear Jim Dalton, we have completed the review of your warning: Anti-social Language in a Public Area and concluded the warning to be in error.
In light of recent evidence provided by multiple parties, the warning has been upgraded to: Serious Anti-social Language in a Public Area Causing Offence.
This warning carries a penalty of 500 citizenship points.
Due to the serious nature of the offence, you have been designated a Disruptive Person.
This designation multiplies the penalty to 1000 citizenship points.
You now have 4012 citizenship points.
You have been reallocated to Level 7.
There is no further appeal to this penalty. To view more details, login to your portal.”  
Jim’s world shook. He sat back down in the chair with a lifeless thud, his ears ringing and stars in his eyes, like he’d just been hit across the back of the head.
He was motionless, staring at the screen.
“This isn’t real,” he thought. “how can this be real?”  
Rosalind, the head of People and Culture, knocked on his office door.
He hadn’t seen her approach. He hadn’t registered anything apart from the message on his phone. Startled, he looked up.
“Hi Jim, I think we’ll just cancel the farewell speech now. If you could make your way out of the office now, please,” Rosalind said, gesturing out the door where two Landis security guards were waiting.
“You’re escorting me out? Really?”
“I think it’s for the best, Jim, if you just go now,” Rosalind said calmly.
Jim was getting angry now. “I’ve been here over three years, and you’re going to treat me like this?”
“Jim, you don’t work for Landis anymore. As of right now, you’re trespassing. I can have these men make you leave, but I think we’d all prefer to avoid that,” Rosalin said with a cold finality.  
Jim felt as if he was in a dream, sleepwalking out of the office. His brand-new suit sat heavy on him, his gold tie screaming of his failure as he walked by colleagues he’d known for years. He looked at them, and they all turned their faces away as he was escorted to the lift.
Rosalind spoke as they waited for the lift. “Jim, these two will escort you to your car and let you out of the car park. It’s my duty to inform you that you are no longer welcome in this building. Please don’t return for any reason. If there are any personal items you’ve left behind, be sure to email my department, and we’ll ensure they are sent out to you.”
That was it, no goodbye or good luck from people he’d worked with for years.
The lift arrived, and Jim and the two security guards stepped into it. Through the closing doors, he could see that Rosalind had already turned her back on him.
#
Jim was sitting in his car silently, the leather seats and luxury trim a welcome familiarity that helped block out the outside world. 
He read and reread the message. It still didn’t seem real. Words kept repeating over and over again in his head.
Multiple sources, Serious Anti-social Language in a Public Area Causing Offence Disruptive Person.
He felt hollow as if his colour has been stripped away from him.
After a few minutes he sighed and decided to deliberately change the way this day was going. He was going to fight this.
Jim dialled Shane’s number.
It didn’t ring, instead it went directly to a recorded message
“We’re Sorry, You Have Reached a Number That Has Been Disconnected or Is No Longer in Service”
What the hell?
He dialled Shane’s number three more times and three more times he heard the same automated message.
“We’re Sorry, You Have Reached a Number That Has Been Disconnected or Is No Longer in Service”
Jim’s newly found resolve cracked a little, Shane and his Lawyer were the first step to reversing all of this.
His personal AI chimed, he was due at Nova Corp in thirty minutes. 
Damn it, Jim realised he’d lost complete track of the time.
He called his new executive assistant.
The call was answered immediately “Nova Corp, Rachael speaking” 
Relieved this call was answered, Jim put on a cheerful voice said “Hi Rachael, It’s Jim Dalton here, just letting you know something came up and I’ll be a few minutes late for my induction meeting, if you could let Rob know please”
Silence on the other end of the call
“Hello?” Jim asked
“I’m so sorry Mr Dalton, I’ve been told to cancel all your appointments, please hold whilst I transfer your call” The line clicked over to hold music before Jim could say a word.
Does, Nova know? Jim thought as the soothing classical hold music played. 
He didn’t have to wait long, the call was answered by another professional female voice “Hello Mr Dalton, you’re speaking with Grace, Mr Hughes Assistant, what can I help you with today?”
Jim was taken a back by the question, it didn’t make any sense.
“Rob Hughes? is Rob available?” Jim asked
“Unfortunately Mr Hughes is unavailable, how can I help you?”
Again with the question, Jim’s mind was turning but unable to comprehended what was going on, he forced himself to clam down
“As I said to Rachael, I’m running a few minutes late for my first day there and just wanted to let Rob know, it took a bit longer than anticipated to say my goodbyes at my my old job” Jim was sweating.
“Mr Dalton, I don’t have you in the schedule for today, are you sure you have the right number?” Grace asked sweetly.
Rob answered his frustration a growing “Look, I’m starting there today as the new VP of Private Client Business, Rob was going to show me around a get me introduced to everyone” 
“I’m sorry Mr Dalton there is nothing on the schedule like that today, I can assure you that if a new VP was coming onboard today I’d know about it, are you sure you know Mr Hughes?” Grace’s voice never wavered from the corporate blandness.
“Well Grace, I can assure you I am starting today, Rob and I had lunch at apex last week and I signed the contract” Jim was starting to loose control.
“Mr Dalton, you’re getting quite agitated, please lower your voice. I’m looking at Mr Hughes calendar right now and he did not have lunch at Apex last week, furthermore a quick check of our staff portal shows no one under the name of Jim Dalton is employed here” Grace seemed to be losing her practiced patience just a little.
They’ve cut me loose, I’m not longer a Level 8 and they are denying I ever was thought Jim.
He didn’t have a response.
Grace asked “Mr Dalton, are you there?”
Jim answered, defeated “Yes, so I’m done there, before I even got started, is that how this is going to play out?”
Grace’s professionalism was back “I’m sorry Mr Dalton, it seems Nova Corp has no record of any dealings with you. Thank you for your time and have a great day!”
Jim didn’t bother hanging up, Grace cut the line as Jim dropped the phone into his lap and slumped back into the driver seat his eyes squeezed shut so hard he was seeing shapes in the darkness, his hand balled into fists pressing against this temples. 
Thankfully the one-way window tint didn’t didn’t allow anyone to see into the car.

#
Jim’s day, and life, was spiralling out of control.
Get it together, now! he told himself
Take stock of the situation and come up with a plan, he’d always backed himself and now wasn’t the time to buckle under the pressure. Where to start though, the job at Novas was gone as was his new Level 8 status. 
At least he was still a Level 7 and if he had to wait out year living like a monk as a Disruptive Person, he could do that. Jim had worked his way up from the lower levels in the Outer, so living as a Level 7 was never going be problem..
Jim was moving forward again, just like his mind was pushing forward with plans he got his car moving too, with nowhere else to go home seemed like a good option right now.
With a destination locked into his EV, Jim could relax a little and consider his situation further. 
His old job at Landis was burnt, but his skills still had to be in demand. He might have to take a few steps back down the corporate ladder, but Jim was sure he’d find a place to recover and quietly work his way back up. After all that’s what the system allowed, you might make a mistake and get a fine or a sanction, but do the right thing, be an outstanding member of society again and all was forgiven. 
Who did he know from before his days at Landis?
Jim was scrolling his contact list when a message came through from Sarah
Fuck, that was not a conversation he wanted to have now.
He had his car read it
“What did you do????? I can’t be around you at the moment, I’m going to my parents until this blows over”
There no way Sarah would have known about the incident at Apex, but she would have been alerted by his change of Status and being named a Disruptive, you can’t hid something like that.
He dictated a text back “Don’t leave, we can sort this out, it’s only a temporary sanction, I’m sure Shane can help smooth it all over, let’s talk when I get back, I’m on my way home now”
Almost immediately there was a reply “THIS WON’T SMOOTH OVER”
Followed by a second text with a just a link.
Jim opened it on the flat panel console in his car, a video started playing. 
His blood ran cold as he realised is was a video of him at the Apex restaurant, drunk and insulting the wait staff in all his new Level 8 arrogance “…Fucking idiot, you’ve just spilled a thousand dollar glass of wine over a contract worth more money than you could even imagine, are you sorry about that…” Jim was waving the wine stained contract right on the waitresses face, the wet pages slapping her “See that, it’s a real job, nothing like you’ll ever get you fucking Troff”
The video paused on close-up of Jim’s face full of rage and righteous indignation and a voice over started in a heavily synthesised voice.
“This is how the so-called Elites treat the workers, sipping expensive wine whilst abusing honest hard working staff who just want to get do thier jobs. Nothing will stop them from belittling and degrading you, These Elite want nothing more holding you all down in the Outer forever. Never forget we outnumber them. We are the Grey”
Mercifully the screen went blank.
Fuck, Fuck, Fuck. If Jim was driving he’d have crashed the car by now.
He texted Sarah back “We can fix this”
The response, again, was instant “No WE can’t, I can’t be around a Disruptive, don’t call me again or I’ll get a restraining order”
Jim was floored, he and Sarah had worked so hard together as a team to climb the ladder. He knew she was super ambitious, but so was he and it was a good match. He didn’t think she’d drop him cold like this.

#
Jim’s EV was a picture of calm, sliding effortlessly through the light early afternoon traffic, leaving the glittering heart of the city with it’s mirrored towers and manicured gardens behind for the urban sprawl of the affluent suburbs of the what locals called the Green. 
No one would have known the turmoil inside.
Jim was slumped in the luxurious leather seat replaying the video of his outburst at Apex over and over. 
Who the hell are the Greys? He’d never heard of them, he tried think back to his time in the Outer after he’d left his parents comfortable, but unambitious, level 5 home to strike out on his own. 
Then it struck him like lightning bolt, he did recognise the name, it was on one of the restricted newscasts he was able to see after Shane’s guy chipped his TV. He’d have  see if he could look it up when he got home. 
All he could remember was they were some sort of loose group of agitators born out of the Level 3’s and 4’s in the Outer who were complaining about thier lot in life. All they had to do was work hard and climb the ladder instead of complaining the newscast commentator had said at the time.
Jim watched it again and again, but not one single piece of footage showed Rob on the other side of the table, it was if someone was filming over Rob’s shoulder, but how could they do that?
His phone pinged again with a message from his PCP
Seriously, what now? Jim thought as he opened the message.
“Dear Jim Dalton, you have been issued a number of warnings: 
(1) Serious Citizenship misconduct: Associating with a Banned Group
(2) Serious Citizenship misconduct: Common Assault
(3) Serious Citizenship misconduct: Drunk and disorderly in a public place
You are hereby summoned to a hearing at the District Citizenship Court, details will be forwarded directly to your lawyer on record.
Due to the serious nature of the offence, your Citizenship Status points have been suspended temporarily and you have been reallocated to Level 5 – Restricted citizen (Temporary) 
To view more details, login to your portal.”
Numb to the constant hits he’d received today Jim just shook his head in disgust. All the plans he’d been making and the effort he’d made step back from the brink were wasted, he was ruined and there was nothing he could do.
As the grim reality of his situation settled on Jim, his car alerted to an issue and gently pulled to the side of the road. 
All I want to do is get home and crawl up into a ball, I don’t need this.
He looked at the console to see what the issue was, a red flashing message was had replaced the slick top of the line interface
[This Vehicle has been disabled by central control.
The registered owner of this vehicle is not longer considered able to fulfil the payment schedule as required.
The financial institution has repossessed this vehicle and an appointed transportation service will arrived at this location to take possession of this vehicle]
The car door opened by itself, almost inviting Jim to leave it. 
Jim just started laughing uncontrollably, a laughter that was tinged with anger and despair at the same time.
He slowly got out of the car, why rush there was nothing left for him to loose.
He was still about 10 miles from his house, normally a 15 min drive, but on foot, he didn’t know.
With a sigh he patted the car a fond farewell and started walking.






CHAPTER 7

SANCTUARY (JIM 4)

Jim was walking, not something he was used to doing, for years he’d only commuted in air-conditioned cars, so walking the 10 or so miles to his house was proving to be harder than he thought.
Just like everything else today, this was not what he expected to be doing, forced to walk home in shame.
Honeslty Jim though, walking with his own thoughts was the best thing he could be doing right now.
Luckily he’d passed out of the city proper before he got kicked out of his car, no his any longer he reminded himself. The hustle and bustle of the city and the busy main roads had been replaced by the Green. It was a rich and verdant suburbia, row upon row of houses that shared the same handful of designers, alternating architectural styles for miles on end, all with small but neat and tidy front gardens that the owners could justifiably be proud of. 
The Green stretched for miles and surrounded the city’s centre like a verdant suburban moat. It was where the bulk of the population lived, Level 6’s and 7’s and for most it was more than enough.
For Jim, now on foot forced to take the time to look at these houses instead of ignoring them as they sped by out the window, it was never enough, his ambition had burned brightly to reach the very top. He had too, if for only a week, then it had all been ripped away from him.
Getting drunk and being rude to wait staff did not justify the sanctions he faced, not even close. He couldn’t help but feel a heavy hand pushing the scales down against him.
He’d taken off his coat and tie and rolled up his sleeves, the afternoon sun was too warm to be out walking, even more so in his expensive tailored suit. The sweat starting to soak his brand new crisp white real cotton shirt, his face flushed and red from the unexpected effort.
The quiet of the walk let Jim think properly for the first time. He had so many questions. 
Who had recorded the footage? He thought Shane’s points lawyer had smoothed it all over, apparently not. What was going on with Shane’s number he wondered, he’d tried calling a few more time with the same message.
Why did the Grey choose him to ruin today? He wasn’t even sure who they were, but they’d taken situation he could handle and blown it up to ruin Jim’s life. 
 Jim was forming another plan as he walked, one way or the other, he was going to find out who did this too him.
He checked his watch, it had been about two hours so far, he reckoned he had about an hour of walking to go before he got home. His once new suit was damp with sweat, thankfully the evening was on it’s way and the sun’s heat was dropping to be replaced by a cooling breeze.
Jim remebered he’d switched his phone to do not disturb when he started walking, he just couldn’t handle any more messages. He took it out of pocket and switched it back, here’s goes nothing he thought with apprehension.
Messages flooded in, five, ten and then twenty and more, text and voice. Finally it stopped and Jim nervously scanned them,searching for anything from Sarah, but there was nothing. Mercifully there was also nothing from his PCP.
There rest were all unknown numbers, cautiously he opened the first one and listened to it
“Hello Jim, can I call you Jim? it Brad here from The Daily Outlet, I just wanted to talk to you about the video of you at Apex, call me back”
Then the next one “Mr Dalton is Sophie here from the News at 7, we’ve got some questions about your association with the Greys, can you tell us how long you’ve been a member? Call me back urgently”
Another one “Hi Jim, it’s Steve here from Citadel News Feed, we’ve love to let you put your side of the story, w’ll give you a whole hour. Please call me back before you call anyone else”
Fuck, Jim’s simply hadn’t anticipated becoming a national news story.
He read some of the texts, all the same, urgent demands for him to call back one media outlet after another.
He recognised a text from his sports club, he knew what it was going to be before he even read it
“Mr Dalton, the board of the The Willow Creek Club and has decided to revoke your membership. Please arrange with the Club Secretary to come in and pick up your personal items.”
Jim had strived for years to get into the Club, it wasn’t a guarantee even when he was a Level 7, it took Jim and Sarah a few years before they’d done enough networking to even get a spot on the waiting list.
He understood now he’d been sudden shunted down to Level 5 he was being stripped of his membership, but it didn’t ease the blow of so much time and effort in getting his membership just being discarded in a two sentence text.
#
Jim continued walking, the well planned suburbia had been replaced by a neat and tidy small shopping village, the kind Jim and Sarah went to every weekend. It wasn’t one of the huge malls that dominated the closer you go to the Outer, this was just for the locals. 
He spotted a connivence store, Yes! he thought.
Jim had forgotten that he’d only had his morning coffee at home,, the sight of the shops and the convenience store instantly reminded him just how thirsty, and hungry he was. He didn’t need to be anywhere right now so a quick strop wouldn’t hurt him.
He was about 30 minutes away from his house and never came to this village so no chance anyone would recognise him. Jim tucked in his shirt, ran his fingers through his hair and tried to make himself as presentable as he could.
Jim walked in and immediately felt the welcome chill of the air-conditioning, he nodded to the cashier how proceeded to completely ignore him. The Convention store was tastefully sized, deliberately just big enough that you could get almost everything you needed without having to venture further afield to a big box supermarket. 
Jim went straight for the drink fridge at the back of the store, his thirst seemed to increase the closer he got. Force of habit had him reaching for a sparkling water, but he paused what was the point, today of all days, to be careful of his calory points tracker. He put back the water and moved over to the soft drink fridge, it had been a while since he’d had a guilt free soft drink. 
He was pondering the flavours when he spotted beer a few fridge doors over, Hell yes! Jim took one cold beer, stopped and took another, it wasn’t as if he had anything to loose today. 
There was TV playing music videos just above the row of fridges and he glanced at it “NEWS ALERT” flashed across the screen.
The new anchor spoke “Interrupting your scheduled programming with this breaking new alert as we follow the story of Jim Dalton, a former Level 8 executive now sanctioned down to Level 5 after the so-called Greys, a little known disruptive group who released the now viral footage of a drunken Dalton abusing and assaulting wait staff in an exclusive restaurant earlier this morning”
The news was showing the footage of Jim at the Apex restaurant in the background.
“We have Tanner Holt on the ground with a breaking update, Tanner what’s new information have you got for us”
Tanner is standing out on the street in front of what looks like Jim’s car “Thanks Lina, as you know we’ve been trying to track down the elusive Mr Dalton to ask him how he is associated with the groups the Greys, and does he have any comment on assaulting wait staff the the Apex restaurant. Mr Dalton looks to have gone to ground for now after leaving his old job at Landis Corpoartion earlier today.
Lina, just moment ago we were alerted by member of the public to this car sitting abandoned here on the side of the road about 10 miles down Trunk Road 4.”
The camera zooms in on Jim’s car, disable on the side of the road with the driver door still open.
Tanner picks up the story again “We’ve since confirmed that this car was registered to Mr Dalton up until only a few hours ago, in fact” He gestures to the man behind the camera “John, get a close-up of the console” 
John obliges and the sames message is flashing on it.
[This Vehicle has been disabled by central control.
The registered owner of this vehicle is not longer considered able to fulfil the payment schedule as required.
The financial institution has repossessed this vehicle and an appointed transportation service will arrived at this location to take possession of this vehicle]
Tanner is now offscreen and the camera stays on the message flashing in red and black on the screen “As you can see here Lina, the car was disabled and repossessed, and it looks like Dalton has just abandoned it here on the side of this busy road, Lina”
“Thanks Tanner, any sign of Mr Dalton?” 
Tanner back on camera and standing next to the open door to Jim’s car “No Lina, no sign of him here, we don’t know where he is at the moment. I’m told that both the Metro Police and the Citizenship Enforcement Bureau have active investigations open into Mr Dalton. Once source from the CEB assured me that Mr Dalton is not in any danger and they are monitoring his movements and will step in if necessary. Back to you Lina
Lina is sitting behind the news desk “This is just another worrying twist in the case of Jim Dalton, a corporate high flyer who who thought the rules didn’t apply to him, we hope he’s safe. Now over to Kira Maddox who is outside Jim Daltons home”
“Kira, any sign of Mr Dalton there?”
“Thanks Lina, I’m standing outside Mr Dalton’s luxury house in the exclusive suburb of Laurelton Park, as you can see behind me there is no sign of activity in the house at the moment, we don’t know of Mr Dalton will even come back here but we’ll be onsite to update if anything happens, back to you Lina”
“Thank you Kira, please do keep us posted. Now back your scheduled programming”
Jim was having what feels like an out of body experience, seeing his car that he was driving only a few hours ago, and his house splashed across the TV, the air-con was now too cold as he shivered with the though if his life being exposed for all to see.
Jim skulked his way to front counter having replaced the beers with a large sugary soft drink. The clerk continued to ignore Jim as he paid, Jim couldn’t get out of there quick enough.
There were at his house, what is he going to do now?
#
The darkness had set in now—cool and enveloping—and it suited Jim’s mood perfectly. He slowed his pace to allow the night to fall. Jim figured he would be less conspicuous walking through the tree-lined streets of the Green.
He silently thanked his ancestors for the total ban on drones. He knew from documentaries about life before the Reclamation that drones could have tracked you anywhere. He shuddered to think what his walk home would have been like with drones following him every step of the way.
The trees provided cover for Jim to sneak quietly up to his street. About 100 metres from his house, Jim cut into one of his neighbours’ front yards and crouched down behind their front hedge. He was able to see through the hedge and observe the circus that was outside the front of his house.
There had to be about fifteen media trucks parked up and down the street. Pop-up gazebos were spewed across the footpath in front of his house, each news outlet vying for the best view of his front yard.
Up here, where Jim was sitting quietly in the hedge, the street was mostly dark, lit by the regularly spaced streetlights, but they didn’t extend their light that far into the front yards under the tres.  
Jim was safe for now and just watched for a while as he considered his next move. Dozens of reporters, producers, and camera operators were milling around, chatting with each other. He could see the lights go on under one of the gazebos as the reporter went live to update their viewers.
The Metro Police had a single patrol car parked just up the street. They’d given over the prime spot to the media, and the two officers were sitting in the car, doing their best to ignore them.
“Damn it!” Jim thought. He’d hoped to avoid the police until he’d been able to find a lawyer willing to work with him. He needed to get home so he could access his personal terminal, where all his old contacts were stored.
Going up to the front door would not work. He could try to sneak around to the back door, but that would mean creeping through his neighbours’ backyards.
“Better than the front door,” Jim thought.  
Keeping low, he moved out of the hedges and towards the side of the house he was hiding in front of. He was four houses over from his own, so this was going to be a real test to get past not only the neighbours but also not tripping any security systems.
He didn’t know the people who lived in this house too well—maybe they’d spoken at a street BBQ once or twice—so he didn’t feel any guilt sneaking through their backyard. Jim slowly and very carefully opened the side gate to the house. He knew it would be open; no one locked their yards in this neighbourhood.
Staying low, he skirted under the windows on the side of the house. At least the curtains were drawn, but the lights were on, and he could hear people moving about and talking inside.
He got past the house and snuck all the way to the very back of the yard, where no light from the house cast any illumination. It was a welcome darkness.
Jim paused. He needed to keep calm and quiet, but his heart was pounding, and the adrenaline was coursing through his veins.  
After a few minutes sitting against the back fence, Jim’s hands had stopped shaking, and he felt ready to move.
“Only three more backyards to cross,” he told himself.
The next obstacle was the six-foot fence. As quickly and quietly as he could, Jim clambered over it and landed softly on the other side. He was fully exposed—the yard was just open lawn. He knew because the Grants’ kids loved soccer, so they’d turned the entirety of their lot into a field. He sprinted across the yard and almost hurdled the next boundary fence.
He could relax here. The Logans were away on holidays, and the house was dark and closed off.
He could see over to his yard—the blare of the bright lights of the media out front creating a surreal halo silhouetting his house.  
“Last one—let’s do this and get home,” Jim thought. He climbed over into the yard of his neighbour and close friend Elliot. The Reeces had moved in almost the same week as Jim and Sarah, and they’d struck up a tight friendship in the years since then.
He was ashamed to admit that he’d given Elliot a bit of a cold shoulder when Shane had taken Jim under his wing.
Jim softly lowered himself to the ground. He knew this backyard well, and he skirted around the back of the pool and the small cabana he’d helped Elliot build when a voice right next to his ear whispered, “Jim.”
Startled, Jim whipped around, ready for anything, to see Elliot lying quietly in the dark on the pool lounger in the cabana.
Elliot chuckled quietly. “You’re a bit jumpy, Jim—especially for someone sneaking through your friend’s backyard.”
Jim had no idea what to say. He blurted out, “It’s not what you think, Elliot…”
Elliot interrupted. “Let’s keep it quiet, Jim. Joanne is in the house with the kids—she thinks I’m out here making sure there were no reported trespassers.”
Jim relaxed a little. “Sorry, Elliot. I didn’t mean to sneak through your yard—it’s just that I wanted to avoid the circus out front.”
“Jim, I don’t care about that. Are you okay, man?” Elliot asked, concern in his voice.
Jim did not feel okay at all. “I’m good—just need to get home and start making some calls to sort all of this out.”
Elliot asked, “Yeah, what’s up with all that? Half the news thinks you’re some sort of rebel in league with the Greys; the other half thinks you’ve been abducted or something.”
Jim was anxious to get home. “I’m just a guy who’s getting screwed right now. None of it’s true—I just need to get home and start fixing it.”
Elliot said, “I get that. You know you can just stay here in the cabana—Joanne will never know; she doesn’t come out here much anyway. I’ve got some food and drink in the fridge—might be safer.”
After a day when everyone was dropping him like a hot stone, Jim was genuinely warmed to hear someone offer help, but he couldn’t risk Elliot’s status by hanging around.
“Thanks, Elliot—really, I mean it—but I’m not going to drag you into this. I’m not a good person to be around right now.”
Elliot shrugged and raised his glass to Jim. “Good luck to you—remember I’m here, buddy.”
Jim gave Elliot a nod and jumped the fence into his backyard.
#
Home finally! Jim was elated, never in his life had it been so hard to just go home. Yet here he was. 
He stood in the darkness of his yard, the low murmur of the media out front only just reaching him, the spotlight lights shining against the front of his house making it seem even darker in the backyard.
Jim, reached into pocket for his keys. They weren’t there.
Oh No he frantically patted down all of his pockets, but his keys were nowhere to be found. Had he left them in the car? Had he dropped them jumping over one of the fences?
He got down on his knees and scrabbled around where he landed to see if he’d only just dropped them. It was too dark to see anything, and after a short time Jim gave up scratching around in the garden. They were gone.
Jim was more annoyed that worried, it would have been too easy just to be able sneaking his back door, this day was really trying to test him. He knew he had a spare set of keys hidden by the side door. The side door felt a bit exposed, he really wanted to get inside without anyone knowing and keep it that way.
Just be careful Jim thought to himself as he tentatively looked around the corner to the side of the house. The picket fence and gate separating the front yard from the side of the house was a good six feet tall, but was only half timbered, so you could easily see through it. 
Jim carefully scanned his path to the side door and it seemed the media pack down by the footpath was at an ebb, no one was really looking in his direction. 
Carefully and slowly, Jim crept around the corner, sliding himself tight against the side of the house he crept the 15 or so meters to the side door. The key was under a pot just across from the door. From the indent of the side door he looked again at the media, no one was paying attention, he snuck over to the pot and knelt down behind it. The pot didn’t provide any cover really, it was only a foot tall and empty. 
Jim gently tilted it and felt under the pot, where was this key? His hand couldn’t find it so he tipped the pot further and bent over to look under.
There it was, stuck to the bottom of the pot with the dirt, it had been a while since he’d needed to use it. Jim grabbed for the key, the pot tipped over too far and he lost control of it. It clattered over and smashed into a dozen or so pieces.
No! The noise sounded deafening to Jim crouched on his knees in the quiet at the side of his house. Panicked he looked up and through the fence. It felt like the media turned as one to look towards the house, the low hum of mundane conversation of the bored media pack rose a pitch. Someone trained a spotlight to where the sound had come from. Jim could see the zebra petter of light and shadow on the ground and him.
He held deathly still, perhaps they couldn’t see him.
A voice yelled out “There’s someone there!” 
Shit, it was a that moment he realised he didn’t have the key in his hand, it was somewhere under the debris of the broken pot and the soil that it held now spread over the side path.
cameramen and reporters were now in a mad dash to his side gate with reporters and camera operators elbowing each other out of the way to get the best shots.
Jim was frantically pushing the broken pieces of terracotta around trying to find the key, he cut his hand on one of the shards and started adding his own blood to the soil.
The first camera man got to the gate stopped to leaned his camera over it and get the shot, but the gate opened and he fell into onto the path directly in front of Jim. Now the gate was open, the pack rushed in with not regard for Jim or the cameraman on the ground.
Jim was light up be a dozen camera mounted lights and the reporters were screaming questions at him at the same time
“Mr Dalton are you a member of the Greys?’
“Mr Dalton why did you assault the waitress?”
“Are you a danger to society”
“What’s it like being designated a disruptive Person?”
Jim’s hand found the key finally and he stood up, his pants covered in mud and dirt, his had red with blood, he eyes squinting starting into the lights.
The throng had surrounded him and were jostling him like he was in a washing machine, he had no choice but to push through them to get to the side door if his house.
Jim pushed but they didn’t move, he pushed again but they were shoulder to should in the tight space.
Jim yelled at them “Get out of my way, get off my property!” and pushed through again. He was hit in the face by one microphone, then another .
A cameraman had backed up into the doorway and Jim yelled again “Move!”
He didn’t.
Jim needed to get inside.
He grabbed cameraman and shoved him are hard as he could off the small stoop of the side door. The man went sprawling into the narrow garden between the side path and the house. His camera flew off onto the pathway smashing into pieces, all the other camera’s capturing the moment the man and the camera hit the ground.
Jim didn’t have time to see any of that, he showed the key into the lock and burst into house slamming the door shut behind him with a force that rattled the windows.
Jim collapsed onto the floor exhausted, he was spent, he didn’t even register the banging on the door as the adrenalin left his body and he passed out lying there on the cold floor in his dark house.




















CHAPTER 8

ESCAPE (JIM 5)

Jim woke up lying face down on the cold dark floor. For a moment he didn’t know where he was, then the realisation of what just happened and the memories of the last twelve or so hours smashed back into his mind with a heavy thud, a shiver racked his body as he lay on the floor.
Then the realisation hit him, he was home!
Jim rolled over on to his back and looked around, he’d ended up on the kitchen floor when he burst in the side door, his face was numb from lying on the cold tiles.
How long had he been there? He checked his watch: just over an hour.
Jim looked at his hands, they were covered in dirt and blood, he picked up more than one cut scrabbling through the broken pot for the keys.
He gingerly got up and went over to the kitchen sink to wash his hands. From the sink he could see through the curtains of the kitchen window to the side of house. The media throng had left, but they’ve left behind trampled bushes and flower beds in the side garden, the path strewn with soil and broken plants and pots. At least someone had closed the gate.
He quietly walked out of the kitchen to the main hell and slowly up to the front door, his curiosity getting the better of him, he needed to see what was going on outside.
Jim peered through the curtains on the door, silently thanking Sarah for spending so much money to make sure no window was left unfurnished. He was actually proud of the work they’d done together in creating such a pleasing interior. It was thier house, but also a prop to host all those networking lunches and dinner parties. The fact the light of the media pack on his front lawn was mostly blocked by the curtains was a blessing.
He could see that at some point the police has set up a line across his front lawn to keep the reporters back on the footpath. There were least twice as many media vans out there now along with what looked like a sizable crowd of onlookers. He was sure his dramatic arrival back home made it even more of a story.
Checking the door was securely locked, he quietly went to the back of house where there was no chance of being seen. He flopped onto the couch in the family room that over looked the backyard and flicked on the TV.
The news was all about him. 
A news anchor was recapping the news and teasing the full bulletin “Former Landis Copration executive who’s verbal abuse and assault of staff at the Apex restaurant was sensationally outed by the anti-establishment group the Grey, was involved in a violent scuffle with reporters at his house earlier this evening. Our full report and the shocking footage in our bulletin at 9pm”
He flicked around channels, they all had the same tease, he didn’t want see it. 
Jim turned the TV off and threw the remote on the couch in disgust. He needed a shower to wash it all off.
Fifteen minutes later Jim had showered and changed, for the first time in what seemed forever, he felt comfortable. Time in the familiar safety of his own house, even besieged as it was by the media, had helped him to think more clearly.
Clean and comfortable he realised he was ravenous and headed for the kitchen to rummage up some food. 
He distracted himself from the weight of all his problems by making a gourmet sandwich, fresh toasted artisan bread, slathered with salted butter, some sliced real turkey and a perfectly ripe soft cheese. Normally he wouldn’t be so extravagant with all the fillings piled high, but this beautiful food, bought just a few days ago as a Level 8 VP at Nova Corp, was going to be his last luxury grocery shop for a long while, so there was absolutely no point waisting it.
He sat back down on the couch with his and thumbed on the TV. He didn’t want to see himself again so he browsed the sports channels to see if there was anything there that could take his mind of his situation. The food and the couch conspired quickly to send him off to sleep, this time he drifted off feeling relaxed and safe.
#
Jim was jolted awake by his phone ringing insistently. He wasn’t sure if it had rung out once or twice. It didn’t matter, he was in no mood to answer it as he lay on the couch groggy and bleary eyed. He let the call ring out again and go to message bank. 
It rang again, immediately. He scrabbled for the phone next to him on the couch and looked to see who was calling – unknown number.
Just another reporter trying to get a scoop Jim thought as he pressed the reject button. He flipped through the phone and saw the same number had called 5 times already, one call after another almost immediately. 
Jim only had time to register this fact when the number called again, he needed to tell whoever it was to stop calling.
“Hello, who is this this? You need to stop calling” Jim said angrily.
The voice was distant and didn’t sound real ”You need to leave your house now”
“Who is this? look I’m not interested in anything you’ve got to offer”
The voice was louder and more insistent “Mr Dalton you may not know us, but we have your best interests at heart, you need to leave your house now”
Jim was baffled by the conversation said “What are you talking about. I’m not going anywhere”
“Turn on the news, you’ve got 5 minutes” The line went dead.
Jim was shook his head, what the fuck was that?
Reflexively he turned on the news the bulletin was well under way, the anchor in the studio was just wrapping up an interview a man sitting in the back of an ambulance.
“….thanks Stephen, I’m glad you’re ok” She looked directly at the camera and continued “That was our Channel 7 Camera man who was earlier this evening assaulted by ex Level 7 executive turned Disruptive Person, Jim Dalton.
Mr Dalton has also caused over 15,000 credits of damage to our news camera.”
She paused listening to something in her ear. “I’m being told by my producers that the network will be pressing charges against Mr Dalton and we’re expecting his arrest imminently”
Jim, jumped up off the couch
NO! He coudn’t go to jail, that would be the end, there was no coming back from that.
Jim was panicking now, the voice on the other end of the phone had been right, he needed to leave, he couldn’t be arrested.
The adrenaline hit brain and the room blurred slightly as he fought to control himself.
Think! he yelled at himself, What did he need? in the back of his mind he knew once he’d left his house he was never coming back.
Money, he needed money. Jim bolted to his office at the front of the house.
Like the rest of the house it was a tastefully decorated prop, guests glancing in would see the dark wood desk sitting in front of custom built in bookcases decorated with books and souvenirs from thier travels and think just how well ready and travelled Jim and Sarah were. Of course it was all Sarahs’ work, Jim had little input when they furnished the office. 
He rushed to the desk and slammed open the top drawer so hard it came clean out of the desk and clattered to the floor it’s contents spilling over the ground. Jim flipped the drawer over and taped to the underside were two small grey items, they looked a lot like a memory sticks from the old days. 
Jim grabbed them and thanked his lucky stars Sarah hadn’t found them and also that he’d kept them all these years.
Jim had grown up in the Outer in a Level 5 household, so he learnt you always had a back-up plan. The sticks were the last inheritance of his parents, two untraceable caches of credits, the sum total of all thier assets in two tiny devices. He’d keep them out of sentimentality more than anything else.
He could see the blue and red flashing lights of the Metro Police bleeding around the edge of the curtains in his office, they must just have pulled up. 
He had to go.
His run to the back door was punctuated by trying to thrust on his runners at the same time. He stopped for a second out of habit looking for the remote to turn off the TV.
The footage of the TV sent a chill down his spine, the voiceover was breathless with anticipation “… as you can see here the Metro Police have arrived in numbers ready to take Jim Dalton into custody” the news coverage showed about a dozen Metro Police officers getting out of thier patrol cars and waving an armoured car through to Jim’s driveway “You can see here the Police are not taking any chances, they don’t know the extend of Daltons’ involvement in the Greys so they are taking every precaution. I’m also being told by a source that they are confident Dalton is still in the house…”
Jim looked at his phone laying next to the remote control, he turned on his heel slipped out his back door.
He immediately bolted to the fence he jumped over hours earlier and leap over into his neighbours backyard. Thankfully Elliot was back inside, the cabana was closed off an quiet, the backyard lights out, he did not want the one person who’d hadn’t shunned him today to get caught up in this.
Jim sprinted across the darkened yard and leapt over the fence to the Logans, the his next neighbour over. He dropped to the ground and he could hear the Police going around the side of his house one yard over, they weren’t trying to be quiet their radios updating thier movements.
He strained to hear what they were saying “Team two is at the rear door, no sign of the suspect, signs of life inside, lights and TV. Holding” a deep voice said in a clipped tone into a radio just loud enough to carry to Jim’s ears.
Jim sat his back against the fence catching his breath for a moment, he had to keep moving and put some distance between him and his house. With purpose he got up and started to more carefully climb over the next fence when a loud voice pierced the night. “Mr Dalton, this is the Metro Police, we have a warrant for your arrest, please come out peacefully”
Damn it! The lights went on in the yard he was just about to jump into, as did the lights in all the houses around him.
He Froze waiting for the light to come on where he was, then he remembered the Grants were away and the yard remained dark.
He was safe for a moment, but he was in a little oasis of darkness in a neighbourhood that was rapidly waking up.
The voice yelled again “Mr Dalton, this is the Metro Police, we have a warrant for your arrest, please come out peacefully, you have 5 minutes to comply” It had to be amplified by the armoured truck.
He was stuck, every house around him was fully awake now.
Then it hit him, the Grants were away and he knew how to get into thier house.
#
Jim remembered a conversation with his neighbour at a BBQ at this house a few months ago. Jason, the owner of the house was complaining about his basement window being ruined by the new sprinkler system he’d just installed. It had warped the frame and he couldn’t shut it properly. He even shown the guys at the BBQ the window.
Jim found it being a small shrub growing up against the back wall, he knelt down and gave it a quick shove and it popped open just like Jason had demonstrated. 
Jim squeezed himself though the small window and dropped to the floor of the basement below with a thud. 
It was pitch black in the basement as he stood and let his eyes become accustomed to the dark. Outside he could hear the loudspeaker again “Mr Dalton we are authorised to enter your house with force, this is you last change to surrender peacefully”
Good Jim thought, they still though he was inside as he gently pulled the window closed jamming it shut into the warped frame.
Luckily houses on this street didn’t alter thier floor plans much being all built by the same developer and Jim was able to feel his way through the basement to the stairs going up to the ground floor. 
He got to the landing at the top of the stairs and stopped cold when he saw the alarm keypad flashing red – active. 
Jim had no idea of the Grant’s alarm code, and he turned around and went back down the stairs deflated, he was stuck down here. The breakers to the house were outside, but that was too dangerous to get to right now with the neighbourhood wide awake and crawling with police and media.
He’d been in this basement before, so he knew there was a tool bench over by the hot water system. Jim carefully felt his way around the basement in the near complete darkness. He almost fell over a suitcase and whacked his head on a low hanging bulkhead that left him seeing stars for a minute, but his hands eventually found the tool banch. 
Carefully he felt around the bench, Yes! he felt a flash light sitting in it’s charger, he snatched it flick the flight on, immediately the stark white light of the LED flashlight illuminated the basement. He scanned the space, standing next to the work bench the room was familiar, the same layout as his own house, but also completely different. 
The owners here had made it more usable than the mostly empty dark space in his own house, his eye settled on the couch, small TV and bar fridge in one corner, apparently Jason spent a bit of time down here.
Jim moved over and opened up the small fridge, inside cool and tempting were some different flavoured soft drinks and a few beers. Jim wondered if Wendy new about the stash of soft drinks and beer? 
He grabbed a soft drink and slumped on the couch, the cool sweet drink quenched his thirst with a stratifying fizzy tingle.
He pick up the remote off to top of the fridge and turned on the small TV mounted on the wall opposite. The room was bathed in a soft multi coloured light and Jim flicked off the flashlight.
The news was on every channel, apparently the appetite for Jim’s downfall hadn’t abated yet, live pictures of the front of his house were splashed across every channel.
 He stopped on one “.. as you can see by the footage here the Metro Police have now been joined by the Citizenship Enforcement Bureau as the operation here goes from arrest to investigation”
The anchor starts talking to a reporter standing in front of Jim house “Over to Tanner Holt who’s been covering this story from the beginning. Tanner what’s the situation there?”
Tanner on the live cross responds “Thanks Lina, about 30 minutes ago Metro Police executed an arrest and search warrant at Jim Dalton’s house here in the affluent suburb of Laurelton Park. Dalton was seen entering his house a few hours earlier and in the result melee assaulted a Channel 7 cameraman and destroyed his camera. When Metro Police entered the house Dalton had already left. Police are tight lipped as to when and how he escaped unnoticed and where he might be at this present time. Metro Police have started a city wide manhunt for Dalton and have warned citizens not to approach him as he could become violent again. Back to you in the studio Lina”
Lina sitting at the news desk “A worrying update indeed, it seems we now have a violent fugitive on the loose in the city, stay safe out there”
Jim sank further into the couch, what was he going to do now? he had to find a way to clear his name.
Jim was shocked out of his thoughts by banging on the front door upstairs “Metro Police, please come to the door”
The Police were here, how did they know where he was?
More banging, louder and more insistent. They couldn’t know, he’d left his phone on the couch.
He switch off the TV and snuck to the basement window to check it was still jammed shut, just as light and voices came into the back yard.
“Neighbours told us no one was home, apparently they’re away on a holiday” said one voice.
“Lucky them, and here we are jumping thier fence to find a fugitive, I know where I’d rather be!” said another voice.
The first voice responded “ There’s nothing here, the house is locked up good an tight, lets move on this neighbourhood isn’t going to question itself”
With that the lights and voice moved away.
Jim’s heart was beating through his chest, but at least they’d confirmed they didn’t know where he was, he’d have to sit tight here whilst the heat died down.

#
Jim woke with a start, he hadn’t even realised he went to sleep on the old couch. It was still dark outside so he couldn’t have been asleep more than a few hours. He sat up feeling a bit groggy, a bit like he had a hangover from the constant shocks and adrenaline dumps of the day before.
At least he’d been safe here for the night, and the couch wasn’t too bad and there’d been no more close encounters with the police or media.
Grabbing another soft drink out of the fridge, Jim randomly thought it was interesting he just wasn’t hungry considered he’d barely had anything to eat yesterday. The cool sugary drink woke him up pleasantly.
He couldn’t stay here though.
He flicked on the TV curious to see what new label they had slapped on him whilst he slept. A different anchor was sitting at the desk, the clock on the screen was showing 5:37am “…nothing new overnight in the case of Jim Dalton, Police are casting a city-wide net this morning…” they were playing image of his house with a much smaller police presence on the screen.
Jim turned it off, the dawn was just breaking, it was now or never.
He need to get the alarm off first, the breaker box was outside at the side of the house. Carefully plying the basement window open Jim climbed out into the frigid pre-dawn air and snuck low and quietly around to the side of the house. 
The box was pretty standard and like everyone else wasn’t locked, there was no need in this neighbourhood. He gently opened it, still door squeaked loudly in the cold silence. He looked around and waited a few breathless moments to see if the noise had attracted any attention, deciding to just get on with it he turned on the flashlight for a second to identify the master switch. 
Jim paused, if this alarm was anything like his cutting off the power would alert the security company to come and inspect the property, he’s have anywhere from 30 minutes to a couple of hours depending where the nearest patrol was. Once he flipped the switch he’d had to move fast.
Jim takes a breath, thinks through his plan one last time and flips the main circuit breaker off. He moves fast back to the basement window and squeezes back through quickly, he’s had some practice now.
He rushed us the basement stairs the check the alarm keypad, it’s dark, no red flashing light, he hopes there is a battery back up somewhere.
Tentatively he opens the door and steps out into the main hallway running up the centre of the house from the from door to the back door. He stands still searching the ceiling. There! he finds the closest alarm sensors across the hall in the kitchen.
Jim, never taking his eye off the sensor walked into the kitchen, the sensor remained dead, no lights flickered. He takes a deep breath of relief, not realising he’d been holding his breath since he step out of the basement. 
The alarm was dead but his didn’t have much time to waste, Where did they keep the car keys?
Jim opened all the kitchen drawers, nothing. He darted across to the study and opened all the desk drawers, nothing either. He tried the drawers on the bookcase behind the desk, the middle one was locked. 
He went back over to the kitchen and grabbed a big carving knife and used it pry open the drawer, splintering the wood as he did so. 
Yes! the car keys.
He can leave now, hopefully no one will notice the car leaving so early in the morning and as the Grant’s are away for at least another week, the car won’t be reported as missing for a while, long enough to give Jim a chance to find a solution.
Jim passes by the kitchen on the way to the garage, he stops and goes back and pilferers some muesli bars and biscuits from the pantry and a big water bottle from the fridge.
Does he need anything else? No he needed to get going.
Jim opened the garage door to see the Grant’s white EV sitting in it, in a dark part of his mind he was half expecting the garage to be empty. Thankfully it wasn’t and the car came alive as the keys came within proximity. The lights turned on, the panel in the interior started up and the drivers door opened. 
The garage door stayed steadfastly closed “Damn it” he’d turned the power off and forgotten about the door. 
He couldn’t go back and turn the power on to open the door, all the sensors would trip and set the alarm off. Jim had no choice but to muscle the door open manually. 
Straining against the inactive motor Jim forced the door up, it took what felt like ages and Jim was sweating by the time the door was up enough to drive the car out. 
Feeling exposed at the entrance to the grager with the light of dawn strengthening by the minute, Jim jumped into the car and drove out.
He was distracted adjusting the seat when the car stopped dead. Jim looked up and standing in front of the car Elliot his neighbour, he was rugged up against the chill morning with his dog max straining on the leash to keep walking.
Elliot looked directly at Jim, visibly shocked at first and then realising what Jim was doing, a wry smile came onto his face. He step back off the driveway and dramatically waved Jim through.
Jim drove past Elliot and out onto the street mouthing the word  “Thanks” through the window. Elliot just gave Jim a mock salute.
The car slid silently out into the street as Jim turned down away from him house, he could see the Police and the media camped out just two doors down, but no one gave the white EV a second glance. His last view before he turned off his street, perhaps for the last time ever, was Elliot standing there chuckling to himself.










CHAPTER 9

CONSEQUENCES (NEIL 3)

It has been a few weeks now since Neil left prison, weeks where he’d just done as he was told, gone where he was told, and followed every rule he’d been given. Partially out of habit, partially because Neil didn’t want to stand out as someone who would create waves.
That had to change today though, what was the point of serving his full sentence only to come out and live almost exactly the same way, rules and curfews, his life was still in a prison, just one with larger boundaries.
He’d been planning his next move ever since he was released. He’d stayed quiet, listened and observed how things worked out here for Level 3’s and he was ready to do something.
Neil had just stepped off the bus from his job in the Rust Belt, the sun had long gone down. Unlike every other night, Neil didn’t immediately go up to his small apartment in the halfway house, tonight he turned away from his building and walked across the street to the brightly lit twenty-four hours supermarket. 
He had no need to go there, he’d stocked up on what he could afford a few days ago, he just needed it to look like he wasn’t going straight to his room for a reason.
Neil browsed the aisles in the shop for a few minutes, pausing every now and then to look out the windows across to his apartment building. Soon enough he could see that there was no one left hanging around after the bus had left, his fellow half-way house residents had all gone inside and no one had followed him into the shop.
He looked at his watch, he had about two hours before curfew so he’d had to move fast. Neil causally moved to the back of the store all the while making sure the cashier was still completely absorbed by his device at the front counter and ignoring the world him.
Quickly he ducked through the entrance to the warehouse in the back, it was just one of those plastic strip barriers, so apart from the muted rustle of the strips, Neil didn’t make a sound. He had figured at this time of night the back of the store would be empty.
